7.10.31 [31. On klematis.] The leaves of klematis are of a pungent and caustic power, so that they even strip away lepra. It would therefore be of the fourth order of heating things, at its beginning. The daphnoides is also called klematis, which some call myrsinoides and some polygonoides. But this is not ulcerating nor pungent, like the one mentioned before; rather it benefits diarrhoea and dysentery when drunk with wine. It soothes pains of the teeth when chewed, and it benefits pains of the womb when applied in a pessary, so far does it fall short of burning or ulcerating, as the former does. But the most admirable Pamphilus, just as he talks nonsense about everything else, writing about things he does not know, so too has run both of these together into one and the same. Dioscorides, however, mentions both in the fourth book, the one simply named klematoeides at the end of the book, the other at the beginning. Hence there is no need for me either to write out further the forms of both, just as I have not done so in the case of the others.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πολυγονοεδὲς, κληματοειδοῦς, ἐς ταὐτόν
7.10.31 [31. On klēmatis.] The leaves of klēmatis are of a pungent and caustic power, so that they even strip off leprai. It would therefore belong to the fourth order of heating things, at its beginning. But the daphnoeides ('laurel-like') is also called klēmatis, which some call myrsinoeides ('myrtle-like') and others polygonoeides ('knotgrass-like'). This, however, is neither ulcerating nor pungent, as the one previously mentioned is; rather, drunk with wine it benefits diarrhoea and dysenteries. Chewed, it soothes pains of the teeth, and applied in a pessary it benefits pains of the womb; so far does it fall short of burning or ulcerating, as the former does. But the most admirable Pamphilus, just as he talks nonsense about everything else, writing about things he does not know, so too he has confused both of these together into one and the same. Dioscorides, however, mentions both in his fourth book: the one simply called klēmatoeides ('vine-like') at the end of the book, the other at the beginning. Hence I too need not describe the forms of the two any further, just as I did not do for the others either.

7.10.35 [λε΄. Περὶ κοκκυμηλέας.] Κοκκυμηλέας ὁ καρπὸς ὑπάγει γαστέρα, καὶ πρόσφατος μὲν ὑπάρχων μᾶλλον, ξηρανθεὶς δ’ ἧττον. Διοσκουρίδης δ’ οὐκ οἶδ’ ὅπως τὰ Δαμασκηνὰ κοκκύμηλα ξηρανθέντα φησὶν ὑπέχειν γαστέρα. ὑπάγει μὲν γὰρ καὶ ταῦτα σαφῶς, ἀλλ’ ἧττον τῶν ἐκ τῆς Ἰβηρίας. ἔστι δὲ τὰ μὲν Δαμασκηνὰ στυπτικώτερα, τὰ δ’ ἐκ τῆς Ἰβηρίας γλυκύτερα, καὶ δὴ καὶ τὰ δένδρα τοῖς καρποῖς ἀνάλογον. ἧττον μὲν στυπτικὰ τὰ κατὰ τὴν Ἰβηρίαν, μᾶλλον δὲ τὰ κατὰ τὴν Δαμασκόν. ἁπλῶς δ’ εἰπεῖν ὧν ἐν τοῖς φύλλοις ἢ τοῖς βλαστοῖς ἐμφαίνεταί τις στύψις σαφὴς, ταῦτα ἀφεψόμενα διάκλυσμα γίγνεται τῶν περὶ γαργαρεῶνα καὶ παρίσθμια φλεγμονῶν. ὁ δὲ τῶν ἀγρίων καρπὸς στυπτικὸς ἐναργῶς ἐστι καὶ σταλτικὸς γαστρός. ὀνομάζεται δὲ τὸ φυτὸν τοῦτο κατὰ τὴν Ἀσίαν προῦμνον. τὸ δὲ κόμμι τοῦ δένδρου φασὶν ἔνιοι μετ’ οἴνου πινόμενον λίθων εἶναι θρυπτικὸν, σὺν ὄξει δὲ λειχῆνας ἰᾶσθαι παίδων, καὶ εἴπερ αὐτὸ ποιεῖ, δῆλον ὡς τμητικῆς τε καὶ λεπτομεροῦς μετέχει δυνάμεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.10.35 [35. On the plum tree.] The fruit of the plum tree loosens the belly, and more so when it is fresh, less when it has been dried. Dioscorides, however, says—I do not know how—that Damascene plums, when dried, check the belly. For these too plainly loosen it, though less than those from Iberia. The Damascene ones are more astringent, those from Iberia sweeter, and indeed the trees too correspond to their fruits: those in Iberia are less astringent, those around Damascus more so. To speak generally, those in whose leaves or shoots some clear astringency is apparent, these, when boiled down, become a mouth-rinse for inflammations about the uvula and tonsils. The fruit of the wild kinds is manifestly astringent and checking of the belly. This plant is called in Asia proumnon. Some say that the gum of the tree, drunk with wine, is capable of breaking up stones, and with vinegar cures the lichen-eruptions of children; and if it does this, it is clear that it partakes of a cutting and fine-particled power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑπέχειν, προῦμνον
7.10.35 [35. On the plum tree.] The fruit of the plum tree loosens the belly, more so when it is fresh, less when it has been dried. On the gourd.] The gourd is of a moist and cold mixture, in the second rank in both respects; whence also the juice of its scrapings is suitable for earaches that arise from inflammation, when it is used together with rose-oil. 7.10.38 [38. On colocynth.] Colocynth is bitter to the taste, but those effects of bitterness which attach to a drug when it is drunk it is unable to perform manifestly, because of the purgative power which it possesses in itself in strong measure, since it is excreted through the belly beforehand together with the things that it purges. The juice of it when green, rubbed in, benefits cases of sciatica.

7.10.38 [38. On colocynth.] Colocynth is bitter in taste, but whatever effects of bitterness belong to a drug that is drunk, these it cannot manifestly produce, because of the purgative power, a strong one, that it possesses in itself: it is excreted through the belly first, together with what it purges. The juice of the green colocynth, rubbed in, benefits cases of ischias.

7.10.42 [μβ΄. Περὶ κονύζης.] Κόνυζα καὶ ἡ μείζων καὶ ἡ μικρὰ παραπλησίας εἰσὶ κράσεώς τε καὶ δυνάμεως, ἐν μὲν τῇ γεύσει δριμεῖαι καὶ πικραὶ φαινόμεναι, θερμαίνουσί τε πάνυ σαφῶς, ἄν γέ τις λειώσας τὰ φύλλα σὺν τοῖς ἁπαλοῖς κλαδίσκοις, θαμνῶδες γὰρ τὸ φυτὸν, ἐπιπλάσσῃ τι μέρος, ἄν τε καὶ ἀφεψήσας ἐν ἐλαίῳ χρήσαιτο τούτῳ. δοκεῖ γοῦν καὶ τῶν κατὰ περίοδον ῥιγῶν ἀλεξητήριον εἶναι τὸ τοιοῦτον ἔλαιον. καὶ τὰ ἄνθη δ’ αὐτῶν παραπλησίας ἐστὶ δυνάμεως, ὥστε τινὲς καὶ ταῦτα σὺν τοῖς φύλλοις τρίψαντες σὺν οἴνῳ ποτίζουσιν ὑπὲρ τοῦ καταμήνια κινῆσαι βιαίως καὶ ἔμβρυα ἐκβαλεῖν. ἔστι δὲ καὶ τρίτον εἶδος κονύζης ἐν ﻿ ὑγροτέροις χωρίοις φυόμενον, ἀμφοῖν δυσωδέστερον καὶ ἀσθενέστερον. ἀλλ’ αἵ γε πρῶται ῥηθεῖσαι τῆς τρίτης εἰσὶ τάξεως ἐν τῷ ξηραίνειν καὶ θερμαίνειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.10.42 [42. On konyza.] Konyza, both the larger and the small, are of similar mixture and power; appearing pungent and bitter to the taste, they heat quite plainly, whether one, having pounded the leaves together with the tender twigs (for the plant is shrub-like), plasters them on some part, or whether, having boiled them down in oil, one uses this. At any rate such an oil seems also to be a protective remedy against the rigors that come by periods. And their flowers too are of similar power, so that some, having ground these as well together with the leaves, give them to drink with wine for the purpose of moving the menses forcibly and expelling embryos. There is also a third kind of konyza, growing in moister places, more foul-smelling and weaker than both. But those mentioned first are of the third order in drying and heating.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
7.10.42 [42. On konyza.] Konyza, both the greater and the small, are of similar mixture and power. To the taste they appear pungent and bitter, and they heat quite clearly, if one pounds the leaves together with the tender sprigs (for the plant is shrubby) and applies them as a plaster to some part, and also if one boils them in oil and uses this. At any rate such an oil is thought to be a remedy against periodic rigors. Their flowers too are of similar power, so that some people pound these together with the leaves and give them to drink with wine in order to move the menses forcibly and to expel embryos. There is also a third kind of konyza, which grows in moister places; it is more ill-smelling and weaker than both of them. But those mentioned first belong to the third order in drying and heating.

7.10.43 [μγ΄. Περὶ κοριάνου.] Κορίανον, ἢ κόριον, ἢ ὅπως ἂν ἐθέλῃς ὀνομάζειν, οἱ μὲν γὰρ Ἕλληνες οἱ παλαιοὶ κορίανον καλοῦσιν, οἱ νεώτεροι δ’ ἰατροὶ πάντες ὀνομάζουσιν κόριον, ὥσπερ καὶ Διοσκορίδης ψυκτικὴν λέγων εἶναι τὴν πόαν οὐκ ὀρθῶς. σύγκειται γὰρ ἐξ ἐναντίων δυνάμεων πολὺ μὲν ἔχουσα πικρᾶς οὐσίας, ἥτις ἐδείκνυτο λεπτομερὴς ὑπάρχειν καὶ γεώδης, οὐκ ὀλίγον δὲ καὶ ὑδατώδους ὑγρότητος χλιαρᾶς κατὰ δύναμιν, ἔχει δέ τι καὶ στύψεως ὀλίγης. ἐξ ὧν ἁπάντων ἐνεργεῖ μὲν ποικίλως ὅσα περ ὁ Διοσκορίδης ἔγραψεν, οὐ μὴν τῷ ψύχειν γε μόνῳ. ἀλλ’ ἐγὼ τὴν αἰτίαν ἑκάστης τῶν κατὰ μέρος αὐτῆς ἐνεργειῶν διηγήσομαι, καίτοι προὔκειτό μοι τὴν ἐμαυτοῦ γνώμην ἐν τῷ παρόντι λόγῳ διέρχεσθαι μόνην. ἀλλ’ ἴσως οὐδὲν κωλύσει, μᾶλλον δ’, εἰ χρὴ τἀληθὲς εἰπεῖν, ὠφελήσει τέ τι καὶ ἀναμνήσει τῶν ἔμπροσθεν μεθόδων τὰ κατὰ τοῦτον τὸν τρόπον ἐπ’ ὀλίγων φαρμάκων λεγόμενα. πρῶτον μὲν οὖν οὐχ ὁ Διοσκορίδης μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἄλλοι πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν ἀδιορίστως ἀποφαίνονται περὶ τῶν νοσημάτων, ὥσπερ ἀμέλει καὶ τῶν νῦν ἔστιν εὑρεῖν οὐκ ὀλίγους ἰατροὺς εὐδοκίμους παρὰ τὰ ἄλλα ταύτῃ μέγιστα σφαλλομένους. ἐνίοτε μὲν γὰρ μέλανος καὶ πελιδνοῦ καὶ ψυχροῦ γεγενημένου τοῦ τὸ ἐρυσίπελας ἐσχηκότος μορίου καὶ δεομένου μηκέτι τῶν ψυχόντων φαρμάκων ὡς πρόσθεν, ἀλλ’ ἤδη τῶν ἐκκενούντων τὸν παρὰ φύσιν ἐμπεπλασμένον ἐν τῷ χωρίῳ χυμὸν, οἵδε ψύχουσιν ἔτι, ἐνίοτε δὲ μεταβαίνουσιν ἐπὶ τὰ διαφοροῦντα, λέγουσι δὲ ἐρυσίπελας ἰᾶσθαι. καὶ δὴ καὶ γράφουσιν ἀρχομένων μὲν ἔτι καὶ αὐξανομένων τῶν ἐρυσιπελάτων ἕτερα φάρμακα, παρακμαζόντων δ’ ἕτερα. τὸ δ’ οὐχ οὕτως ἔχει. οὔτε γὰρ ἐρυσίπελας ἔτι κλητέον, ὅταν οἴχεται τὸ φλογῶδες αὐτοῦ καὶ ζέον καὶ χολῶδες, οὔτε ψυχρὰ ταῖς δυνάμεσιν, οὔτε τὰ τῶν τοιούτων διαθέσεων ἰάματα νομιστέον, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ εἰ καὶ κατ’ ἀρχὰς εὐθὺς, ἢ πληγέντων, ἢ ὁπωσοῦν ἄλλως, ὄγκος ἐν μορίῳ τινὶ πελιδνὸς ἢ μέλας ἐγεγένητο καὶ ψυχρὸν ἂν ἐνομίζετο πάθος εἶναι καὶ δεῖσθαι τῶν διαφορούντων τε καὶ θερμαινόντων φαρμάκων, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν εἶναι τρόπον εἰ μεταπέσοι ποτὲ θερμὴ διάθεσις εἰς ψυχρὰν, ἐπιλαθέσθαι μὲν τῆς προτέρας, ὀνομάζειν δ’ ἑτέρῳ τὴν δευτέραν ὀνόματι, ἢ εἰ μὴ τό γε ὄνομα μεταβάλλειν ἐθέλοις, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ ἔνιοι τῆς μὲν ἀρχῆς ἕτερα, τῆς παρακμῆς δ’ ἄλλα γράφουσι βοηθήματα, μήτοι γε καὶ ψυχρὰ νομίζειν εἶναι τὰ τῆς παρακμῆς φάρμακα, συγχωρητέον μὲν ἐρυσίπελας ὀνομάζειν, εἴ τις βούλοιτο, τὴν τοιαύτην διάθεσιν, οὐ μὴν θερμήν γ’ ἔτι καλεῖν αὐτὴν συγχωρητέον, ὁπότε κατέψυκται. μὴ τοίνυν μηδὲ τὸ τηνικαῦτα βοηθοῦν αὐτῇ ψυκτικὸν εἶναι νομίζωμεν, ὥσπερ ὁ Διοσκορίδης τὸ κορίανον, ὅτι καταπλαττόμενον,ὡς αὐτός φησι, μετ’ ἄρτου ἢ ἀλφίτων ἰᾶται τὸ ἐρυσίπελας. ἀκριβὲς γὰρ ἐρυσίπελας, ὃ δὴ φλογῶδές τε καὶ ξανθόν ἐστιν, οὐκ ἄν ποτε ἰάσαιτο σὺν ἄρτῳ κορίανον, ἀλλὰ τὸ ἀποψυχόμενον ἤδη. διὰ τοῦθ’ ἡμεῖς ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν λόγοις, ὁπότε τῇ διωρισμένῃ πείρᾳ δοκιμάζειν ἑκάστου τῶν φαρμάκων τὴν δύναμιν ἠξιοῦμεν, ἁπλοῦν ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα τὸ πάθος ἐκλέγεσθαι συνεβουλεύομεν. οἱ πολλοὶ δὲ τῶν ἰατρῶν οὐδ’ ὅτι τὰ πλεῖστα τῶν νοσημάτων εὐθὺς ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς εἰσβάλλει σύνθετα γιγνώσκουσιν, οὐδὲ ὡς ἄλλο μέν τι πάθος ἐρυσίπελάς ἐστιν ἀκριβὲς, ἄλλο δ’ ἡ καλουμένη πρὸς ἁπάντων ἡμῶν ἤδη συνήθως, ἢ πρός γε τῶν παλαιῶν, φλεγμονή. ἄλλα δ’ ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ πάμπολλα τὰ μὲν ὡς ἂν εἴποι τις ἐρυσιπέλατα φλεγμονώδη, τὰ δ’ οἶον φλεγμοναί τινες ἐρυσιπελατώδεις. εὕροις δ’ ἄν ποτε καὶ μηδέτερον ἐπικρατοῦν, ἀλλ’ ἀκριβῶς ἰσάζοντα, καίτοι καὶ οἰδηματῶδες ἐνίοτε φαίνεται γιγνόμενον ἐρυσίπελας, ὥσπερ ἕτερον σκιῤῥῶδες, ὥστε καὶ τῶν συνθέτων νοσημάτων οὐκ ὀλίγον ἀθροίζεσθαι πλῆθος. ὑπὲρ ὧν ἐπὶ πλέον μὲν ἐν ἄλλοις τέ τισιν εἴρηται καὶ τοῖς τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου γράμμασι, λεχθήσεται δὲ καὶ νῦν, τό γε τοσοῦτον ἀναγκαῖον αὐτῶν μνημονεῦσαι, ὡς ἀκριβὲς ἐρυσίπελας οὐκ ἄν ποτε τὸ προειρημένον ἰάσαιτο κατάπλασμα. καλῶ δ’ ἀκριβὲς, ἐπειδὰν ὑπὸ χολώδους ῥεύματος ἐμπλησθῇ τὸ μόριον. ὅτι δὲ πόῤῥω τοῦ ψύχειν ἐστὶ κορίανον, ἔνεστί σοι κᾀξ αὐτῶν ὧν Διοσκορίδης ἔγραψε καταμαθεῖν. χοιράδας γοῦν φησι διαλύειν αὐτὸ μετ’ ἐρεγμοῦ. ὅτι δ’ οὐδὲν τῶν ψυχόντων φαρμάκων ἱκανόν ἐστι διαλύειν χοιράδας, οὐδ’ αὐτὸν οἶμαι τὸν Διοσκορίδην ἀμφισβητήσειν μυρία μὲν ἀναγράψαντα φάρμακα χοιράδων ἰατικὰ, πάντα δ’ ὁμολογοῦντα καὶ θερμὰ ταῖς κράσεσι καὶ διαφορητικὰ ταῖς ἐνεργείαις ὑπάρχειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παρὰ τὰ ἄλλα, διωρισμένῃ πείρᾳ
7.10.43 [43. On coriander.] Coriander (korianon), or korion, or however you wish to name it — for the ancient Greeks call it korianon, whereas all the more recent physicians name it korion, as does Dioscorides, who says, not correctly, that the herb is cooling. For it is composed of opposite powers, having much bitter substance, which was shown to be fine-particled and earthy, and also not a little watery moisture that is lukewarm in power; and it also has something of a slight astringency. From all of these it acts in the various ways that Dioscorides wrote of, but certainly not by cooling alone. But I shall set out the cause of each of its particular actions, even though I had proposed in the present treatise to go through my own opinion only. Yet perhaps nothing will prevent it — rather, if the truth must be told, what is said in this manner about a few drugs will both be of some benefit and will recall to mind the methods set out earlier. First, then, not only Dioscorides but many other physicians too make pronouncements about diseases without drawing distinctions, just as indeed among those of the present day one can find not a few physicians of good repute who, beyond all else, go most gravely astray in this respect. For sometimes, when the part that has had the erysipelas has become black and livid and cold and no longer needs the cooling drugs as before, but now those that evacuate the humour lodged contrary to nature in the region, these men still cool it; and sometimes they pass over to the dispersing drugs, and yet say they are curing erysipelas. And indeed they write some drugs for cases of erysipelas that are still beginning and increasing, and others for those in decline. But this is not how it is. For it must no longer be called erysipelas when its fiery, boiling and bilious character has departed, nor must the remedies for such conditions be considered cold in their powers; but just as, if right at the beginning, whether from a blow or in any other way whatever, a livid or black swelling had arisen in some part, it would have been considered a cold affection and one needing dispersing and heating drugs, in the same way, if a hot condition should ever change into a cold one, one should forget the former and name the second by another name; or, if you should not wish to change the name but, as some do, to write some remedies for the beginning and others for the decline, you should by no means consider the drugs of the decline to be cold: it must be granted that one may name such a condition erysipelas, if one wishes, but it must not be granted that one still call it hot, once it has been cooled down. Let us not, then, consider what helps it at that stage to be cooling, as Dioscorides does coriander, because, applied as a plaster, as he himself says, with bread or barley meal it cures erysipelas. For coriander with bread would never cure a strict erysipelas, that is, one which is fiery and yellow, but only one that is already cooling off. For this reason we, in the preceding books, when we required that the power of each of the drugs be tested by discriminated experience, advised choosing an affection as simple as possible. But the majority of physicians do not know even that most diseases attack as composite right from the start, nor that strict erysipelas is one affection and another is what is now habitually called by all of us, or at any rate by the ancients, phlegmone. And in between there are very many others, some, as one might say, phlegmonous erysipelases, others as it were erysipelatous phlegmonai. You might sometimes also find neither predominating but the two exactly balanced; and indeed erysipelas sometimes appears to become oedematous, as another becomes scirrhous, so that no small number of composite diseases is accumulated. About these more has been said both in certain other works and in the writings of the Therapeutic Method, and it will be said now too, since it is necessary to mention them at least to this extent: that the aforesaid plaster would never cure a strict erysipelas. I call it strict when the part has been filled with a bilious flux. And that coriander is far from cooling, you can learn even from the very things Dioscorides wrote. For he says that it dissolves scrofulous swellings (choirades) with bean meal. But that none of the cooling drugs is capable of dissolving scrofulous swellings, I think not even Dioscorides himself would dispute, having recorded countless drugs curative of scrofulous swellings, all of which he agrees are both hot in their mixtures and dispersing in their actions.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐρεγμοῦ, πρὸς ἁπάντων ἡμῶν ἤδη συνήθως, ἢ πρός γε τῶν παλαιῶν, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν εἶναι τρόπον, διωρισμένῃ πείρᾳ, χοιράδας
7.10.43 [43. On coriander.] Coriander (koriannon), or korion, or however you wish to name it: for the ancient Greeks call it koriannon, while the more recent physicians all name it korion, as does Dioscorides, who says, incorrectly, that the herb is cooling. For it is composed of contrary powers. It has much bitter substance, which was shown to be fine-particled and earthy, and not a little watery moisture, lukewarm in power, and it also has something of a slight astringency. From all of these it brings about, in varied ways, the effects that Dioscorides wrote of, but certainly not by cooling alone. But I shall set out the cause of each of its particular actions, even though my intention in the present account was to go through my own judgement only. Perhaps, though, nothing will prevent this; rather, if one must speak the truth, what is said in this manner about a few drugs will be of some benefit and will serve as a reminder of the methods set out earlier. First, then, not Dioscorides alone but also many other physicians make pronouncements about diseases without drawing distinctions, just as, indeed, even among present-day physicians one can find not a few who are well regarded in other respects but who err very greatly in this. For sometimes, when the part that has had the erysipelas has become black, livid and cold, and no longer needs cooling drugs as before, but now needs those that evacuate the humour unnaturally impacted in the region, these people still cool it. At other times they pass on to dispersing drugs, yet they say that they are curing erysipelas. And indeed they also prescribe some drugs while the cases of erysipelas are still beginning and increasing, and others when they are declining. But this is not how things are. For neither should it still be called erysipelas once its fiery, seething and bilious character has gone, nor should the remedies for such conditions be thought cold in their powers. Suppose that right at the start, whether from a blow or in any other way, a livid or black swelling had arisen in some part: it would have been thought to be a cold affection and to need dispersing and heating drugs. In the same way, if a hot condition should at some point change into a cold one, it is right to forget the former and to name the second by another name. Or, if you would not wish to change the name, but, as some do, write some remedies for the beginning and others for the decline, then at least you should not think that the drugs of the decline are also cold. One must grant that someone may call such a condition erysipelas if he wishes, but one must not grant that he still call it hot, once it has been chilled. Let us therefore not think either that what helps it at that time is cooling, as Dioscorides thinks coriander is, because, as he himself says, when applied as a poultice with bread or barley-groats it cures erysipelas. For an exact erysipelas, which is fiery and yellow, coriander with bread would never cure; it cures only one that is already cooling off. For this reason, in the earlier books, when we required that the power of each drug be tested by qualified experience, we advised choosing an affection as simple as possible. But most physicians do not know even that most diseases attack as compound right from the beginning. Nor do they know that exact erysipelas is one affection, and that which is now customarily called by all of us, or at any rate by the ancients, phlegmone is another. In between there are very many others: some one might call phlegmonous cases of erysipelas, others, as it were, certain erysipelatous phlegmonai. You may sometimes also find neither prevailing, but the two exactly balanced. Indeed, erysipelas sometimes appears to become oedematous, as another becomes scirrhous, so that a not inconsiderable multitude of compound diseases is also gathered. About these things more has been said both in certain other works and in the books of the Therapeutic Method, and something will be said now as well. At least this much of them it is necessary to recall: the aforementioned poultice would never cure exact erysipelas. I call it exact when the part has been filled by a bilious flux. That coriander is far from cooling you can learn even from what Dioscorides himself wrote. He says, at any rate, that together with crushed pulse (eregmos) it resolves choirades. That none of the cooling drugs is capable of resolving choirades, not even Dioscorides himself, I think, will dispute. He recorded countless drugs that cure choirades, all of which are agreed to be hot in their mixtures and dispersing in their actions.

7.10.44 [μδ΄. Περὶ κορωνόποδος.] Κορωνόποδος ἡ ῥίζα πεπίστευται κοιλιακοὺς ὠφελεῖν ἐσθιομένη. 7.10.45 [με΄. Περὶ κόστου.] Κόστος βραχείας μὲν πάνυ τῆς πικρᾶς, πλείστης δὲ τῆς δριμείας καὶ θερμῆς μετέχει ποιότητός τε καὶ δυνάμεως, ὡς ἤδη καὶ ἑλκοῦν, ὅθεν ἀνατρίβουσιν αὐτῷ μετ’ ἐλαίου τὸ σῶμα πρὸ τῆς καταβολῆς ἐπὶ τῶν κατὰ περίοδον ῥιγούντων. οὕτω δὲ κᾀπὶ τῶν ἰσχιαδικῶν καὶ παραλελυμένων τε καὶ ὅλως ἐφ’ ὧν ἤτοι θερμῆναί τι πρόκειται μόριον, ἢ ἐκ τοῦ βάθους εἰς τὴν ἐπιφάνειαν ἕλξαι τινὰ χυμὸν, ἐπὶ τὸν κόστον ἔρχονται. διὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν αἰτίαν οὐρητικός τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ ἐμμήνων ἀγωγὸν καὶ πρὸς ﻿ ῥήγματά τε καὶ σπάσματα καὶ πόνους πλευρῶν ἁρμόττει. διὰ δὲ τὴν συνοῦσαν πικρότητα καὶ πλατείας ἕλμινθας ἀναιρεῖ, δι’ ἥνπερ καὶ πρὸς τὰς ἐφήλεις αὐτῷ χρῶνται μεθ’ ὕδατος ἢ μέλιτος. ἐνυπάρχει δ’ αὐτῷ τις τῇ κράσει φυσώδης ὑγρότης, δι’ ἣν καὶ πρὸς ἀφροδίσια παρορμᾷ μετ’ οἰνομέλιτος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.10.44 [44. On korōnopous.] The root of korōnopous is believed to benefit those with coeliac affections when eaten. 7.10.45 [45. On kostos.] Kostos partakes very slightly of the bitter quality and power, but very largely of the pungent and hot one, so that it even ulcerates; for which reason they rub the body with it, together with oil, before the onset of the attack in those who shiver periodically. In the same way they also resort to kostos in cases of sciatica and paralysis, and in general wherever the aim is either to heat some part or to draw some humour from the depth to the surface. For the same reason it is also diuretic and draws the menses, and is suitable for ruptures and sprains and pains of the sides. Through the bitterness that accompanies it, it also destroys flat worms, and on account of this they also use it for epheleis with water or honey. There is also present in its mixture a certain flatulent moisture, on account of which it also incites to sexual intercourse when taken with honeyed wine.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σπάσματα, ἑλκοῦν
7.10.44 [44. On coronopus.] The root of coronopus is believed to benefit coeliacs when eaten. 7.10.45 [45. On costus.] Costus has a very small share of the bitter quality and power, but a very large share of the pungent and hot, so that it is already even ulcerating. For this reason they rub the body with it, together with oil, before the onset [of the paroxysm] in those who suffer rigors periodically. In the same way they also turn to costus in cases of sciatics and the paralysed, and generally in those cases where the aim is either to heat some part or to draw some humour from the depth to the surface. For the same reason it is also diuretic and brings on the menses, and it is suitable for ruptures and sprains and pains of the sides. Because of the bitterness present in it, it also kills flat worms; on account of this same bitterness they also use it for ephelides, with water or honey. There is also present in its mixture a certain flatulent moisture, on account of which it also stimulates to sexual intercourse when taken with honeyed wine [oinomeli].

7.10.46 [μστ΄. Περὶ κοτυληδόνος.] Κοτυληδὼν μικτῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως ὑγρᾶς, ὑποψύχρου καὶ τινὸς ἀμυδρῶς ὑποστυφούσης καὶ σὺν αὐτῇ βραχείας πικρᾶς, ὅθεν ἐμψύχει καὶ ἀποκρούεται καὶ ῥύπτει καὶ διαφορεῖ. φλεγμονάς τε οὖν τὰς ἐρυσιπελατώδεις καὶ ἐρυσιπέλατα φλεγμονώδη θεραπεύει, καὶ μάλιστα στομάχου καυσουμένου χρήσιμον ἱκανῶς ἐστιν ἐπίπλασμα. πεπίστευται δὲ τὰ φύλλα σὺν τῇ ῥίζῃ ἐσθιόμενα λίθους τε θρύπτειν καὶ οὖρα κινεῖν.

7.10.46 [46. On kotyledon.] Kotyledon is of a mixed power—moist, somewhat cold, and with a certain faintly astringent quality, and together with this a slight bitter one—whence it cools and repels and is cleansing and dispersing. It therefore treats erysipelatous inflammations and inflammatory erysipelas, and it is an especially and quite useful plaster for a stomach that is burning. The leaves eaten together with the root are believed to break up stones and to move urine.

7.10.46 [46. On kotyledon.] Kotyledon is of a mixed power: a moist, somewhat cold one, and a certain one that is faintly somewhat astringent, and with it a slight bitter one; hence it cools, repels, cleanses and disperses. It therefore treats erysipelatous inflammations and inflammatory erysipelas, and it is a quite useful plaster especially when the stomach is burning. The leaves, eaten together with the root, are believed both to break up stones and to provoke urine.

Περὶ κρανίας.] Κρανίας τοῦ δένδρου καὶ ὁ καρπὸς στρυφνὸς ἱκανῶς ἐστιν ἐδώδιμος ὑπάρχων, ὥστε οὐδὲν θαυμαστὸν ἕλκειν καὶ ἰσχυρῶς ἐπέχειν αὐτὸν τὴν γαστέρα, καθάπερ καὶ τὰ μέσπιλα. καὶ τὰ φύλλα δὲ καὶ οἱ βλαστοὶ στρυφνοὶ γευόμενοί εἰσι καὶ ξηραίνουσιν ἰσχυρῶς, ὥστε καὶ τὰ μέγιστα τραύματα κολλᾷν δύνανται καὶ μάλιστα τῶν σκληρῶν σωμάτων. μικροῖς δὲ τραύμασιν ἢ μαλακοῖς σώμασιν ἐναντίοι μᾶλλόν εἰσιν, συντείνουσι γὰρ ταῦτα ξηραίνοντα ἐπιπλέον τοῦ δέοντος.

7.10.47 [47. On the cornel.] Of the cornel tree, the fruit too is quite acerbic, although it is edible, so that it is not at all surprising that it draws together and strongly checks the belly, just as medlars do too. And the leaves and the shoots too are acerbic to the taste and dry strongly, so that they are able to glue together even the largest wounds, and especially those of hard bodies. But for small wounds or soft bodies they are rather adverse, for they put these under strain, drying them to a greater degree than is required.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἕλκειν, συντείνουσι
7.10.47 [47. On the cornel (krania).] Of the cornel tree, the fruit too is quite acerbic, although it is edible, so that it is no wonder that it draws and strongly holds back the belly, just as medlars also do. The leaves and the shoots too are acerbic to the taste and dry strongly, so that they are able to agglutinate even the largest wounds, and especially those of hard bodies. But to small wounds or soft bodies they are rather contrary, for they draw these tight, drying them to a greater degree than is needed.

7.10.48 [μη΄. Περὶ κράμβης.] Κράμβη ἐδώδιμος ξηραντικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, ἐσθιομένη τε καὶ ἔξωθεν ἐπιτιθεμένη. οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πως δριμείας ἱκανῶς, ἀλλ’ ὥστε καὶ τραύματα κολλᾷ καὶ τὰ κακοήθη τῶν ἑλκῶν ἰᾶται, καὶ τὰς φλεγμονὰς τὰς ἤδη σκιῤῥουμένας τε καὶ δυσλύτους καὶ τῶν ἐρυσιπελάτων τὰ τοιαῦτα. κατὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν ἐπινυκτίδας καὶ ἕρπητας ἰᾶται. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ ῥυπτικὸν ἐν αὑτῇ, καθ’ ὃ καὶ λέπρας θεραπεύει. τὸ δὲ σπέρμα πινόμενον ἕλμινθας ἀναιρεῖ καὶ μάλιστα τῆς Αἰγυπτίας κράμβης, ὅσῳπερ καὶ ξηροτέρα τὴν κρᾶσίν ἐστι, καὶ δηλονότι πικρᾶς μετέχει ποιότητος τὸ σπέρμα, ὥσπερ καὶ τἄλλα πάντα φάρμακα τὰ πρὸς ἕλμινθας ἁρμόττοντα. κατὰ τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν ἐφήλιδάς τε καὶ φακοὺς ὅσα τε ἄλλα μετρίας δεῖται ῥύψεως ὀνίνησιν. οἵ τε καυλοὶ τῆς κράμβης καυθέντες ἰσχυρῶς ξηραίνουσαν ποιοῦσι τέφραν, ὡς ἤδη τι καὶ τῆς καυστικῆς μετέχειν δυνάμεως. κατὰ τοῦτ’ οὖν αὐτῇ καὶ μιγνύντες στέαρ παλαιὸν εἴς τε τὰ τῶν πλευρῶν ἀλγήματα χρόνια κᾂν εἴπου τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον εἴη χρῶνται. διαφορητικὸν γὰρ ἰσχυρῶς ἀποτελεῖται τὸ φάρμακον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: γέ πως, κᾂν εἴπου τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον εἴη
7.10.48 [48. On cabbage.] Edible cabbage is of a drying power, both when eaten and when applied externally. It is not, however, as yet in any way sufficiently pungent, but is such that it both glues wounds together and heals the malignant kind of ulcers, and inflammations that are already becoming scirrhous and hard to resolve, and those erysipelata that are of this sort. By the same power it heals epinyctides and herpetes. It also has something cleansing in it, by virtue of which it also treats leprai. The seed, when drunk, destroys worms, and most of all that of the Egyptian cabbage, inasmuch as it is also drier in its mixture; and it is clear that the seed partakes of a bitter quality, just like all the other drugs that are suited against worms. By the same power it benefits ephelides and lentigines (phakoi) and whatever else requires moderate cleansing. The stalks of the cabbage, when burned, produce a strongly drying ash, so that it even partakes already to some degree of the caustic power. In accordance with this, then, they also mix old fat with it and use it for chronic pains of the sides and for anything else of this kind, if there should be such; for the drug is rendered strongly dispersing.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πως δριμείας ἱκανῶς, ἀλλ’ ὥστε, κᾂν εἴπου, φακοὺς
7.10.48 [48. On krambē.] Edible krambē is of a drying power, both when eaten and when applied externally. It is not, however, in any way yet considerably pungent, but only to the extent that it unites wounds and heals the malignant among ulcers, and inflammations that are already becoming scirrhous and hard to resolve, and the like among erysipelata. By the same power it heals epinyktides and herpētes. It also has something cleansing in it, in virtue of which it also treats leprai. Its seed, when drunk, destroys worms, and especially the seed of the Egyptian krambē, inasmuch as this is also drier in its mixture; and evidently the seed partakes of a bitter quality, as do all the other drugs suited against worms. By the same power it benefits ephēlides and phakoi and whatever else requires moderate cleansing. And the stalks of krambē, when burnt, produce a strongly drying ash, so that it already partakes somewhat of the caustic power as well. On this account, then, they also mix old fat with it and use it for chronic pains of the sides, and wherever there may be any other such thing. For the drug is rendered strongly dispersing.

7.10.49 [49. On wild cabbage.] Wild cabbage is hotter and drier in mixture than the cultivated, just as practically all other wild plants too are stronger in both powers than the cultivated plants of the same kind. Hence it is not even taken within the body without distress, since it has departed further from the human mixture. For this very reason it is also more bitter to those who taste it than the cultivated kind. For the cultivated kind too has something slightly bitter and pungent, but the wild has each of these in a strong form, and for this reason it both disperses and cleanses more vigorously than the cultivated.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)

For the cultivated too has something somewhat bitter and pungent in it, but the wild has each of these strongly; hence it also disperses and cleanses away more vigorously than the cultivated.

7.10.53 [νγ΄. Περὶ κριθῶν.] Κριθαὶ τῆς πρώτης εἰσὶ τάξεως ἐν τῷ ξηραίνειν τε καὶ ψύχειν, ἔχουσι δέ τι καὶ ῥυπτικὸν ὀλίγον. εἰσὶ δὲ τοῦ τῶν κυάμων ἀλεύρου τοῦ χωρὶς τοῦ λέμματος βραχεῖ τινι ξηραντικώτεραι. τὰ δ’ ἄλλα πάντα παραπλήσια χρωμένοις ἔξωθεν. ἐσθιόμεναι δὲ ταύτῃ πλεονεκτοῦσι κυάμων, ὅτι τὸ φυσῶδες ἀποτίθενται. κυάμῳ δὲ ὅπως ἂν ἑψηθῇ παραμένει τὸ φυσῶδες. παχυμερεστέρας γάρ ἐστιν οὐσίας ἢ κατὰ κριθὴν, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τροφιμώτερος αὐτῆς ἐστιν. ἐπεὶ δ’ ὀλίγον ἄμφω τῆς μεσότητος ἀποκεχωρήκασι, διὰ τοῦτό εἰσι πολύχρηστοι. τὰ γὰρ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα πολλὰ ἑτέροις μίγνυται, καθάπερ τινὲς ὗλαι, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ὁ κηρὸς καὶ τὸ ἔλαιον οὐκ ὀλίγοις ἐπιπλέκεται φαρμάκοις. τὰ δ’ ἄλφιτα πολὺ καὶ τῶν κριθῶν αὐτῶν ἐστι ξηραντικώτερα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πολλὰ, ὗλαι
7.10.53 [53. On barley.] Barley is of the first order in drying and cooling, and it also has some slight cleansing property. It is by some small degree more drying than bean-flour from which the husk has been removed. In all other respects it is similar for those who use it externally. When eaten, however, it has the advantage over beans in this respect, that it puts off its flatulent quality; whereas in the bean, however it is boiled, the flatulent quality persists. For it is of a more coarse-particled substance than barley, and for this reason it is also more nourishing than barley. And since both have departed only a little from the mean, for this reason they are of manifold use. For drugs of this kind are mixed to a great extent with others, like certain materials, and for this reason wax and oil too are combined with not a few drugs. But barley-groats are much more drying even than barley itself.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὗλαι, πολλὰ
7.10.53 [53. On barley.] Barley is of the first order in drying and cooling, and it also has a slight cleansing quality. It is a little more drying than bean meal without the husk; in all other respects the two are similar when used externally. When eaten, however, barley has this advantage over beans: it lays aside its flatulent quality, whereas in the bean, however it is boiled, the flatulent quality remains. For the bean is of a more coarse-particled substance than barley, and for this reason it is also more nourishing than barley. And since both have departed only a little from the mean, for this reason they are of many uses. For such drugs are often mixed with others, as a kind of base material, and for this reason wax and oil too are combined with not a few drugs. Barley groats are much more drying even than barley itself.

7.10.54 [νδ΄. Περὶ κρίμνου.] Κρίμνον. τὸ παχυμερὲς οὕτως ὀνομάζεται τοῦ τε πυρίνου καὶ τοῦ ἐκ τῶν ζειῶν ἀλεύρου. ἔστι δὲ τροφιμώτερον μὲν ἀλφίτου, δυσπεπτότερον δέ. καλεῖται δὲ τὸ ἐξ αὐτοῦ ῥόφημα πολτὸς, καὶ ἔστιν ὁ ἀπὸ τῆς ζειᾶς στατικὸς ἠρέμα τῆς κοιλίας καὶ μᾶλλον εἰ φρυχθείη.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.10.54 [54. On krimnon.] Krimnon. This is the name given to the coarse-particled part both of wheat flour and of the flour from zeia. It is more nourishing than barley meal, but harder to digest. The gruel made from it is called poltos, and the one from zeia is gently binding to the belly, and more so if it has been roasted.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φρυχθείη
7.10.54 [54. On krimnon.] Krimnon. This is the name given to the coarse-particled part both of wheaten flour and of the flour made from zeia. It is more nourishing than barley-groats, but harder to digest. The gruel made from it is called poltos, and the one from zeia is gently binding to the belly, and more so if the krimnon has been parched.

7.10.55 [νε΄. Περὶ κρίνου.] Κρίνου τὸ μὲν ἄνθος κράσει μικτόν ἐστιν ἐκ λεπτομεροῦς τέ τινος καὶ γεώδους οὐσίας, ἐξ ἧσπερ ἔχει καὶ τὴν ἐν τῇ γεύσει πικρότητα, καί τινος ὑδατώδους εὐκράτου. ὅθεν καὶ τοὔλαιον τὸ ἐξ αὐτοῦ καὶ τὸ μύρον ἀδήκτου διαφορητικῆς τε καὶ μαλακτικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, ἐξ ὧνπερ καὶ ταῖς ἐν μήτραις σκληρότησιν ἐπιτηδειότατόν ἐστιν, καὶ ἡ ῥίζα δὲ καὶ τὰ φύλλα καθ’ ἑαυτὰ λειούμενα ξηραίνει καὶ ῥύπτει καὶ διαφορεῖ μετρίως. οὕτω γοῦν κᾀπὶ τῶν κατακαυμάτων ἁρμόττει. δεῖται γὰρ καὶ ταῦτα ﻿ μετρίως ξηραντικῆς τε ἅμα καὶ ῥυπτικῆς δυνάμεως. τὴν μὲν οὖν ῥίζαν ὀπτήσαντες, εἶθ’ ἅμα ῥοδίνῳ λειώσαντες ἐπιτιθέασι τοῖς κατακαύμασιν ἄχρι συνουλώσεως. ἔστι γὰρ καὶ ἄλλως ἁπάντων ἑλκῶν εἰς ἐπούλωσιν ἀγαθὸν φάρμακον. οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ καὶ ὑστέρας μαλάττει καὶ καταμήνια προκαλεῖται. τὰ δὲ φύλλα προσέψοντες ἐπιτιθέασιν καὶ αὐτὰ μέχρι κατουλώσεως οὐ τοῖς καύμασι μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἕλκεσιν. καὶ ταριχεύουσι δέ τινες ἐν ὄξει καὶ χρῶνται τραυματικοῖς. πλεῖον δ’ ἐν τῇ ῥίζῃ τῆς ῥυπτικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως ἤπερ ἐν τοῖς φύλλοις, οὐ μὴν οὐδ’ ἐν ταύτῃ πολὺ, καθὰ προείρηται· τῆς πρώτης γάρ εἰσι τάξεως τῶν ῥυπτικῶν φαρμάκων, ὅθεν ὅταν ἀλφοὺς ἢ ψώραν ἢ λέπραν ἢ ἀχῶρας ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἀποῤῥῦψαι βουληθῶμεν, ἐπιμίγνυμεν αὐτῇ τῶν ἄλλων τι τῶν ἰσχυροτέρων φαρμάκων, οἷόν πέρ ἐστιν καὶ τὸ μέλι. συμμέτρως δὲ αὐτῇ τοῦτο μιχθὲν καὶ πρὸς τὰς τῶν νεύρων ἁρμόττει διαιρέσεις καὶ πρὸς τὰ ἄλλα σύμπαντα ὅσα δεῖται ξηραίνεσθαι σφοδρῶς ἄνευ δήξεως. ἀπεθέμεθα δέ ποτε καὶ τὸν τῶν φύλλων χυλὸν, ἑψήσαντες σὺν ὄξει καὶ μέλιτι. πενταπλάσιος δ’ ἦν ὁ χυλὸς ἑκατέρου καὶ ἦν εὐδόκιμον τὸ φάρμακον εἰς πάντα τὰ ξηραίνεσθαι σφοδρῶς δεόμενα χωρὶς τοῦ δάκνεσθαι, καθάπερ ὅσα τε τραύματα μεγάλα καὶ μάλιστα κατὰ τὰς κεφαλὰς γίγνεται τῶν μυῶν, ὅσα τε πλαδαρὰ καὶ χρόνια καὶ δυσεπούλωτα τῶν ἑλκῶν ἐστι. ταυτὶ μὲν οὖν ἤδη τῆς περὶ συνθέσεως φαρμάκων πραγματείας ἐστὶ, ἧς ἀπέχεσθαι μὲν ὡς οἷόν τε πειρῶμαι τό γε νῦν εἶναι, πολλάκις δ’ αὐτῆς τῶν πραγμάτων ἡ φύσις ἀναγκάζει προσάπτεσθαι, καὶ ἀποχωρεῖν τε χρὴ ταχέως αὖθις αὐτῶν ἐπανέρχεσθαί τε πρὸς τὰ νῦν ἡμῖν προκείμενα.

7.10.55 [55. On lily.] Of the lily, the flower is mixed in its temperament, from a certain fine-particled and earthy substance, from which it also has its bitterness in taste, and from a certain well-tempered watery substance. Hence both the oil made from it and the perfume are of a non-biting dispersing and softening power, as a result of which it is also most suitable for hardnesses in the womb; and the root and the leaves, ground fine by themselves, dry and cleanse and disperse moderately. So at any rate it is also fitting for burns; for these too require a power that is moderately drying and at the same time cleansing. Accordingly, having roasted the root and then ground it fine together with rose-oil, they apply it to burns until cicatrization. For it is also in other respects a good drug for bringing all ulcers to cicatrization. Moreover, it also softens the womb and draws forth the menses. The leaves they boil beforehand and apply, these too, until cicatrization, not only to burns but also to the other ulcers. And some also preserve them in vinegar and use them as wound-remedies. There is more of the cleansing power in the root than in the leaves, yet not much even in this, as has been said before; for they belong to the first rank of cleansing drugs, which is why, when we wish to cleanse away alphos or psora or lepra or achores or anything of that kind, we mix in with it one of the other, stronger drugs, such as honey is. And when this is mixed with it in due proportion it is also fitting for divisions of the sinews and for all the other things that need to be dried vigorously without biting. We once also stored away the juice of the leaves, having boiled it with vinegar and honey; the juice was five times the amount of each of the two, and the drug was well esteemed for everything that needs to be dried vigorously without being bitten, such as those large wounds that occur especially at the heads of the muscles, and those ulcers that are flabby and chronic and hard to cicatrize. These matters, however, already belong to the treatise on the composition of drugs, from which I try to keep away as far as possible for the present, but the nature of the subject often compels one to touch upon it, and one must quickly withdraw from these things again and return to what now lies before us.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προσέψοντες, ἐξ ὧνπερ, ἀπεθέμεθα, ἀποχωρεῖν τε χρὴ ταχέως αὖθις αὐτῶν
7.10.55 [55. On the lily.] The flower of the lily is, in its mixture, compounded of a certain fine-particled and earthy substance, from which it also has its bitterness in taste, and of a certain well-tempered watery substance. Hence the oil made from it and the perfume are likewise of a non-biting, dispersing and softening power, and on account of these they are also most suitable for hardnesses in the uterus. The root and the leaves, ground smooth by themselves, dry, cleanse and disperse moderately. In this way, at any rate, they are suited also to burns, for these too require a power that is moderately drying and at the same time cleansing. So they roast the root, then grind it smooth together with rose oil and apply it to burns until they close with a scar. For it is in other respects too a good drug for bringing all ulcers to cicatrization. Not only this, but it also softens wombs and calls forth the menses. The leaves they boil lightly and apply them too, until they have scarred over, not only to burns but also to other ulcers. Some also preserve them in vinegar and use them as wound remedies. There is more of the cleansing power in the root than in the leaves, yet not much even in it, as was said before; for they belong to the first order of cleansing drugs. Hence, whenever we wish to cleanse away alphoi or psora or lepra or achores or anything of that kind, we mix with it some other of the stronger drugs, such as honey is. This, mixed with it in due proportion, suits both severings of the nerves and all other things that need to be dried strongly without biting. We once also stored up the juice of the leaves, having boiled it with vinegar and honey. The juice was fivefold the amount of each, and the drug was highly esteemed for all things that need to be dried strongly without being bitten, such as wounds that are large, and especially those occurring at the heads of the muscles, and those ulcers that are flabby, chronic and hard to cicatrize. These matters, however, already belong to the treatise on the composition of drugs, from which I try to abstain as far as possible for the present; yet the nature of the subject often compels me to touch upon it, and one must quickly withdraw from these matters again and return to what is now before us.

7.10.56 [νστ΄. Περὶ κροκοδειλίου.] Κροκοδειλίου τὸ σπέρμα δριμὺ μέν ἐστι καὶ ἀρωματίζον οὐρητικόν τε καὶ καταμηνίων κινητικὸν, ὥστε καὶ θερμῆς ἂν εἴη δυνάμεως καὶ διαφορητικῆς καὶ ξηραντικῆς. καὶ ὁ χυλὸς δὲ τοῦ τε καυλοῦ καὶ τοῦ σπέρματος ὁμοίας ὢν δυνάμεως μὲν ὠφελεῖ τοὺς νεφριτικούς. ἡ δὲ ῥίζα ταῖς τοῦ θώρακος ἀναπτύσεσιν ἰσχυρῶς συνεργεῖ, δριμεῖα μὲν ἧττον ὑπάρχουσα τοῦ σπέρματος, πικρὰ δ’ οὐχ ἧττον οὖσα. ἄγει δὲ καὶ διὰ ῥινῶν αἷμα.

7.10.56 [56. On krokodeilion.] The seed of krokodeilion is pungent and aromatic, diuretic and a mover of the menses, so that it would be of a hot power, both dispersing and drying. The juice, too, of the stalk and of the seed, being of a similar power, benefits those suffering from kidney disease. The root strongly assists the expectorations from the chest, being less pungent than the seed, but no less bitter. It also draws blood through the nostrils.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Κροκοδειλίου, ἀρωματίζον, μὲν
7.10.56 [56. On krokodeilion.] The seed of krokodeilion is pungent and aromatic, and diuretic and stimulating of the menses, so that it would also be of a hot power, and a dispersing and drying one. The juice too, both of the stem and of the seed, being of a similar power, benefits nephritics. The root strongly assists expectorations from the chest, being less pungent than the seed, but no less bitter. It also draws blood through the nostrils.

7.10.57 [νζ΄. Περὶ· κρόκου.] Κρόκος ἔχει μέν τι καὶ στῦφον ὀλίγον, ὅπερ ἐδείχθη γεῶδες ψυχρόν. ἐπικρατεῖ δ’ ἐν αὐτῷ θερμαίνουσα ποιότης τε καὶ δύναμις, ὥστε τὴν ὅλην οὐσίαν αὐτοῦ τῆς δευτέρας μὲν εἶναι τῶν θερμαινόντων τάξεως, τῆς πρώτης δὲ τῶν ξηραινόντων, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ πεπτικὸν ἔχει τι, συντιμωρούσης εἰς τοῦτο καὶ τῆς βραχείας στύψεως. οἷς γὰρ ἂν μὴ σφοδρῶς θερμαίνουσι φαρμάκοις ὀλίγη προσῇ στύψις, ἶσον δύναται ταῖς ἐμπλαστικαῖς καλουμέναις οὐσίαις, ἃς, ὅταν θερμότητι συνυπάρχωσι, μὴ σφόδρα πεπτικὰς ἐδείκνυμεν ὑπάρχειν.

7.10.57 [57. On saffron.] Saffron has indeed also something slightly astringent, which was shown to be earthy and cold; but what predominates in it is a heating quality and power, so that its whole substance is of the second order of heating things and of the first of drying things, and for this reason it also has something concocting, the slight astringency too contributing to this. For in drugs that do not heat vehemently, when a small astringency is present in addition, it has the same power as the substances called clogging, which, when they coexist with heat, we showed to be not vehemently concocting.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συντιμωρούσης, μὴ σφόδρα πεπτικὰς (the argument seems to require 'concoctive when they coexist with a heat that is not vigorous'; the text as printed reads 'not very concoctive'), Περὶ·
7.10.57 [57. On saffron.] Saffron has something astringent in it too, though only a little, and this was shown to be earthy and cold. But a heating quality and power prevails in it, so that its whole substance belongs to the second order of heating things and to the first of drying things. For this reason it also has something concoctive about it, the slight astringency also helping towards this. For in drugs that do not heat vigorously, if a little astringency is present, they have the same power as the substances called clogging, which, when they coexist with heat, we showed to be not very concoctive.

7.10.58 [νη΄. Περὶ κρομμύου.] Κρόμμυον ἐκ τῆς τετάρτης ἐστὶ τάξεως τῶν θερμαινόντων. ἡ δ’ οὐσία παχυμερής ἐστιν αὐτοῦ μᾶλλον, ὅθεν καὶ τὰς αἱμοῤῥοΐδας ἀναστομοῖ προστιθέμενον καὶ σὺν ὄξει καταχριόμενον ἐν ἡλίῳ τοὺς ἀλφοὺς ἀποῤῥύπτει καὶ παρατριβόμενον ἀλωπεκίαις θᾶττον ἀλκυονίου παρορμᾷ τὰς τρίχας. εἰ δ’ ἀποχωρίσειεν αὐτοῦ τις τὸν χυλὸν, ὅσον μὲν ὑπόλοιπον ἱκανῶς ἐστι γεώδους οὐσίας θερμῆς, αὐτὸς δ’ ὁ χυλὸς ὑδατώδους τε καὶ ἀερώδους θερμότητος. οὕτω οὖν καὶ τοὺς ὑποχεομένους καὶ ἀμβλυώττοντας ἐπὶ πάχει χυμῶν ὀνίνησιν ὑπαλειφόμενος. ἐκ δὲ τῆς τούτου κράσεως ὅλον τὸ κρόμμυον φυσῶδές ἐστιν ἐσθιόμενον, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ὅσα ξηρότερα τὴν κρᾶσιν ἀφυσότερα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑποχεομένους
7.10.58 [58. On onion.] Onion is of the fourth order of heating things. Its substance, moreover, is rather coarse-particled, whence also, when applied, it opens the mouths of haemorrhoids, and, smeared on with vinegar in the sun, it cleanses away alphoi, and, rubbed on alopecias, it stimulates the hairs more quickly than alcyonium. But if one separates its juice from it, what remains is to a considerable degree of a hot earthy substance, while the juice itself is of a watery and airy heat. Thus, then, when smeared on beneath the eyelids it also benefits those with hypochyma (suffusion) and those whose sight is dulled on account of thickness of humours. And from the mixture of this, the whole onion is flatulent when eaten, and for this reason those that are drier in mixture are less flatulent.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὕτω οὖν (possibly corrupt for οὗτος οὖν, 'this [juice], then', agreeing with masculine ὑπαλειφόμενος; rendered with that sense), ὑποχεομένους (rendered 'those suffering from suffusion', i.e. hypochysis), ὅσον μὲν ὑπόλοιπον ἱκανῶς ἐστι (syntax loose; rendered 'what remains is abundantly'), προστιθέμενον (rendered 'applied as a suppository'; could be simply 'applied')
7.10.58 [58. On onion.] Onion belongs to the fourth order of heating things. Its substance is rather coarse-particled; hence, applied as a suppository, it opens haemorrhoids; smeared on with vinegar in the sun it cleanses away alphoi; and rubbed on alopeciae it stimulates the hairs more quickly than alcyonium. If one separates the juice from it, what remains is abundantly of an earthy, hot substance, while the juice itself is of a watery and airy heat. So, then, when anointed on, it [the juice] also benefits those suffering from suffusion and those with dim sight owing to thickness of humours. From the mixture of this [juice], the onion as a whole is flatulent when eaten; and for this reason those that are drier in mixture are less flatulent.

7.10.59 [νθ΄. Περὶ κυάμου.] Κύαμος καθ’ ἑκάτερα τῆς μέσης κράσεώς ἐστιν, ἐγγυτάτω κατὰ τὸ ξηραίνειν καὶ κατὰ τὸ ψύχειν. μετέχει δέ τινος ἐπ’ ὀλίγον καὶ ῥυπτικῆς δυνάμεως ἡ σὰρξ αὐτοῦ, καθάπερ τὸ λέμμα τῆς στυπτικῆς, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τῶν ἰατρῶν ἔνιοι τὸν κύαμον ὅλον ἑψήσαντες σὺν ὀξυκράτῳ τοῖς δυσεντερικοῖς καὶ τοῖς κοιλιακοῖς καὶ τοῖς ἐμετικοῖς ἔδοσαν. ἔστι δὲ ὡς ἔδεσμα μὲν εἴπερ τι καὶ ἄλλο φυσῶδές τε καὶ δύσπεπτον, ἀναπτύσεσι μέντοι ταῖς ἐκ θώρακός τε καὶ πνεύμονος ἐπιτήδειον. ὡς φάρμακον δ’ ἔξωθεν ἐπιτιθέμενον ἀλύπως ξηραίνει. ἐπὶ μέντοι ποδαγρικοῖς ἐχρησάμεθα πολλάκις αὐτῷ δι’ ὕδατος ἑψήσαντες, εἶτα μίξαντες ὕειον στέαρ. ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν κατὰ νεῦρα θλασμάτων τε καὶ ἑλκῶν δι’ ὀξυμέλιτος τὸ ἄλευρον ἐπεθήκαμεν· ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἤδη φλεγμαινόντων ἐκ πληγῆς σὺν ἀλφίτοις. καὶ διδύμων δ’ ἐστὶ καὶ μαστῶν ἀγαθὸν κατάπλασμα, φιλεῖ γὰρ ταῦτα τὰ μόρια μετρίως ψύχεσθαι φλεγμαίνοντα, καὶ μάλιστα, ὅταν ἐκ γάλακτος ἐν αὐτοῖς τυρωθέντος οἱ μαστοὶ φλεγμαίνωσι. τοιγαροῦν καὶ τὸ γάλα σβέννυται πρὸς τοῦ τοιούτου καταπλάσματος, ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ τῶν παίδων ἐφήβαιον ἐπιπλασσόμενον ἀλεύρῳ κυαμίνῳ μέχρι πλέονος ἄνηβον διαμένει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.10.59 [59. On the bean.] The bean lies on either side of the middle mixture, very close to it both in respect of drying and in respect of cooling. Its flesh also participates to a slight degree in a certain cleansing power, just as the husk does in an astringent one, and for this reason some physicians, having boiled the whole bean with oxykraton, have given it to dysenteric patients, to those with coeliac affections and to those prone to vomiting. As a food it is flatulent and hard to digest if ever anything was, though it is suitable for expectorations from the chest and lung. As a drug applied externally it dries without causing distress. Indeed we have often used it on gouty patients, boiling it in water and then mixing in pig's fat. On contusions and wounds in the region of the sinews we have applied the flour with oxymel; and on parts already inflamed from a blow, with barley-meal. It is also a good plaster for the testicles and the breasts, for these parts, when inflamed, like to be cooled moderately, and especially when the breasts are inflamed because the milk has curdled within them. Accordingly the milk too is quenched by such a plaster, just as the pubic region of children, when plastered over with bean flour, remains without pubic hair for a longer time.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καθ’ ἑκάτερα τῆς μέσης κράσεώς, ἐγγυτάτω, ἐμετικοῖς, σβέννυται
7.10.59 [59. On the bean.] The bean is, on each side, of the middle mixture, lying very close to it as regards drying and as regards cooling. Its flesh also shares, to a small degree, in a certain cleansing power, just as the husk shares in the astringent one; and for this reason some physicians have boiled the whole bean with oxycrate and given it to dysenteric patients, to coeliacs and to those prone to vomiting. As a food it is flatulent and hard to concoct, if anything else is, yet it is suitable for expectorations from the chest and lung. As a drug applied externally it dries without causing distress. For gouty patients, however, we have often used it, boiling it in water and then mixing in pig's fat. For contusions and ulcers in the region of the nerves we have applied the meal with oxymel, and for parts already inflamed from a blow, with barley groats. It is also a good poultice for the testicles and the breasts, for these parts, when inflamed, are disposed to be cooled moderately, and especially when the breasts are inflamed from milk curdled in them. Accordingly the milk too is quenched by such a poultice, just as the pubes of boys, when plastered with bean meal, remains hairless for longer.

7.10.60 [ξ΄. Περὶ κυκλαμίνου.] Κυκλάμινος ποικίλος τὴν δύναμίν ἐστι· καὶ γὰρ καὶ ῥύπτει καὶ τέμνει καὶ ἀναστομοῖ καὶ ἐπισπᾶται καὶ διαφορεῖ. δῆλον δ’ ἐκ τῶν κατὰ μέρος ἔργων. ὁ μὲν γὰρ χυλὸς αὐτῆς αἱμοῤῥοΐδας ἀναστομοῖ καὶ προκαλεῖται βιαίως εἰς ἀπόπατον, ἐν κροκύδι προστιθέμενος. οὕτω δὲ καὶ ταῖς τὰ φύματα καὶ τὰς χοιράδας ἁπάσας τε τὰς ἄλλας σκληρότητας διαφορούσαις δυνάμεσι μίγνυται καὶ τοῖς ὑποχεομένοις ἁρμόττει, μετὰ μέλιτος ὑπαλειφόμενος, καὶ πρὸς τούτοις ἔτι διὰ ῥινῶν καθαίρει. σφοδρὰ δ’ οὕτως ἐστὶν ἡ δύναμις αὐτοῦ, ὥστε καὶ τοῦ ἐπιγαστρίου καταχριομένου ﻿ κοιλίαν ὑπάγει καὶ ἔμβρυα διαφθείρει, καὶ γὰρ καὶ ἄλλως φθόριον ἰσχυρὸν ἐν πεσσῷ προστιθέμενος. ἡ δ’ ὅλη ῥίζα τοῦ χυλοῦ μέν ἐστιν ἀσθενεστέρα, σφοδρὰ δ’ ἐστὶ καὶ αὐτή. καὶ γὰρ ἔμμηνα κινεῖ πινομένη τε καὶ προστιθεμένη καὶ ἰκτεριῶντας ὀνίνησιν, οὐ μόνον ἐκκαθαίρουσα τὸ σπλάγχνον, ἀλλὰ τὴν ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ σώματι χολὴν ἐκκρίνουσα δι’ ἱδρώτων. ὅθεν ἐπὶ τῇ πόσει συνεργεῖν δεῖ παντὶ τρόπῳ τῇ τῶν ἱδρώτων γενέσει. χρὴ δ’ εἶναι τὸ πινόμενον πλῆθος αὐτῆς ἄχρι τριῶν δραχμῶν, ἤτοι μετὰ γλυκέος ἢ μελικράτου. ῥύπτει δὲ καὶ τὸ δέρμα, κατά τε τὰ ἄλλα κᾀν τῷ τὰς ἀλωπεκίας καὶ τὰς ἐφηλίδας, ἅπαντά τε τὰ ἐξανθήματα θεραπεύειν. ὀνίνησι δὲ καὶ τοὺς σκληροὺς σπλῆνας ἐπιπλαττομένη πρόσφατός τε καὶ ξηρά. διδόασι δ’ ἔνιοι τὴν ξηρὰν ῥίζαν καὶ τοῖς ἀσθματικοῖς. ἡ δ’ ἑτέρα κυκλάμινος, ἣν ἔνιοι κισσάνθεμον ὀνομάζουσι, διὰ τὸ κισσῷ κατὰ τὸ ἄνθος ἐοικέναι, τὴν ῥίζαν ἄχρηστον ἔχουσα τὸν καρπὸν ἔχει δραστικὸν ἱκανῶς, ὥστε καὶ μετ’ οἴνου λευκοῦ κυάθων δυοῖν πινόμενος ἐπὶ πλείους ἡμέρας ἰᾶται σπλῆνας, κινῶν οὖρα καὶ τὴν κοιλίαν ὑπάγων, ἐκβάλλει δὲ καὶ τὰ χόρια καὶ τοῖς ἀσθματικοῖς ἀρήγει. ἡ ποιότης δ’ αὐτοῦ γευομένῳ δριμεῖά τε καὶ ὑπόγλισχρός ἐστι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑπαλειφόμενος, κισσάνθεμον
7.10.60 [60. On cyclamen.] Cyclamen is varied in its power; for it both cleanses and cuts and opens up and draws out and disperses. This is clear from its particular effects. For its juice opens up haemorrhoids and forcibly provokes evacuation of the bowel when applied on a tuft of wool. So too it is mixed with the powers that disperse phymata and scrofulous swellings and all the other hardnesses, and it is suited to those suffering from cataract, being smeared on beneath the lids with honey, and in addition to these it also purges through the nostrils. So violent is its power that even when the epigastrium is anointed with it, it draws down the belly and destroys embryos; and indeed in other ways too it is a strong abortifacient when applied in a pessary. The whole root is weaker than the juice, but it too is violent. For it moves the menses, both when drunk and when applied, and it benefits those suffering from jaundice, not only cleansing out the viscus but also excreting the bile in the whole body through sweats. Hence after the draught one must assist in every way the production of sweats. The quantity of it that is drunk should be up to three drachms, either with sweet wine or with melikraton. It also cleanses the skin, both in other respects and in treating the alopecias and the ephelides and all the exanthemata. It also benefits hard spleens when plastered on, both fresh and dry. Some give the dry root also to asthmatics. The other cyclamen, which some call kissanthemon because it resembles ivy in its flower, while it has a useless root has a fruit sufficiently active that, when drunk with two kyathoi of white wine for several days, it heals spleens, moving urine and drawing down the belly; and it also expels the afterbirth and helps asthmatics. Its quality to one tasting it is pungent and somewhat viscous.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑποχεομένοις, καταχριομένου, κισσάνθεμον, ὑπόγλισχρός, κυάθων δυοῖν
7.10.60 [60. On cyclamen.] Cyclamen is varied in its power: for it cleanses, cuts, opens the mouths of vessels, draws, and disperses. This is clear from its particular effects. Its juice, applied on a flock of wool, opens the mouths of haemorrhoids and forcibly provokes to stool. In the same way it is also mixed into the powers that disperse tumours (phymata), scrofulous swellings (choirades) and all other hardnesses. Smeared on with honey, it is suited to those suffering from cataract (hypochysis), and in addition to these it also purges through the nostrils. Its power is so vehement that even when the epigastrium is anointed with it, it moves the bowels and destroys embryos; for it is in any case a strong abortifacient when applied in a pessary. The whole root is weaker than the juice, but it too is vehement. For it moves the menses when drunk and when applied, and it benefits the jaundiced, not only by thoroughly purging the viscus but also by excreting the bile in the whole body through sweats. Hence, upon the drinking of it, one must assist the generation of sweats in every way. The amount of it drunk should be up to three drachms, with either sweet wine or melikraton. It also cleanses the skin, both in other respects and in treating alopecias, ephelides and all eruptions. It also benefits hard spleens when applied as a plaster, whether fresh or dry. Some also give the dry root to the asthmatic. The other cyclamen, which some call kissanthemon because it resembles ivy in its flower, has a useless root but a sufficiently active fruit, so that when drunk with white wine, in an amount of two cyathi, for several days, it heals spleens, moving the urine and loosening the bowels; it also expels the afterbirths and helps the asthmatic. Its quality to one who tastes it is pungent and somewhat viscid.

7.10.63 [ξγ΄. Περὶ κυπαρίσσου.] Κυπαρίσσου τὰ φύλλα καὶ οἱ βλαστοὶ καὶ τὰ σφαιρία τὰ νέα καὶ μαλακὰ μεγάλων τραυμάτων ἐν σκληροῖς σώμασίν ἐστι κολλητικά. ἐξ οὗ δῆλον ὡς ξηραντικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, οὐδὲν ἐπιφανὲς ἐχούσης δριμὺ καὶ θερμὸν, ὥσπερ οὖν καὶ ἡ γεῦσις μαρτυρεῖ· φαίνεται γὰρ ἐν αὐτῇ βραχὺ μέν τι τὸ δριμὺ, πλεῖστον δὲ τὸ πικρὸν καὶ πολὺ πλέον ἔτι τὸ στρυφνὸν ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ φυτῷ. τοσοῦτον δ’ ἐστὶν ἐν αὐτῇ τὸ δριμὺ καὶ τό θερ μὸν, ὅσον ποδηγεῖν μὲν τὴν στρυφνότητα πρὸς τὸ βάθος, οὐδεμίαν δ’ αὐτὸ θερμότητα καὶ δῆξιν ἐργάζεσθαι τοῖς σώμασιν. διὰ τοῦτο τὰς κατὰ τὸ βάθος ὑγρότητας ἐν ταῖς πλαδαραῖς καὶ σηπεδονώδεσι διαθέσεσιν ἀλύπως τε ἅμα καὶ ἀσφαλῶς ἐκβόσκεται, τῶν θερμαινόντων καὶ ξηραινόντων ἐκδαπανᾷν μὲν τὰς περιεχομένας δυνάμεων, ἑτέρας δ’ ἐπισπωμένων τῇ δριμύτητι καὶ θερμότητι. οὕτω δὲ καὶ τοὺς ἐντεροκηλικοὺς ὠφελεῖ. καὶ γὰρ ξηραίνει καὶ τόνον ἐντίθησι τοῖς δι’ ὑγρότητα χαλαροῖς σώμασιν, ὡς ἂν τῆς στύψεως εἰς βάθος καταδυομένης, τῷ ποδηγεῖσθαι πρὸς τῆς μεμιγμένης αὐτῇ θερμότητος, εἰς τοσοῦτον ἡκούσης μέτρον, ὡς ποδηγεῖν μὲν δύνασθαι, δάκνειν δ’ οὐδέπω δύνασθαι. χρῶνται δ’ αὐτῇ τινες καὶ ἐπ’ ἀνθράκων καὶ ἑρπήτων, ἀλφίτοις μιγνύντες, ὡς ἐκδαπανώσῃ χωρὶς τοῦ θερμαίνειν τὰς ἐργαζομένας ὑγρότητας τὰ τοιαῦτα νοσήματα. ἔνιοι δὲ καὶ πρὸς ἐρυσιπέλατα χρῶνται, μιγνύντες ἀλφίτου μεθ’ ὕδατος ἢ ὀξυκράτου ὑδαροῦς.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σφαιρία, ἐκβόσκεται
7.10.63 [63. On cypress.] Of cypress, the leaves and the shoots and the little globes, when young and soft, are agglutinating of large wounds in hard bodies. From this it is clear that it is of a drying power, one having nothing conspicuously pungent and hot, as indeed the taste also bears witness; for there appears in it some small amount of the pungent, a very great amount of the bitter, and still much more of the acerbic throughout the whole plant. And there is only so much of the pungent and the hot in it as to guide the acerbity toward the depth, while itself producing no heat and biting in bodies. For this reason it feeds upon the moistures lying in the depth in flabby and putrefactive conditions painlessly and at the same time safely, whereas the heating and drying powers do indeed consume the moistures that are contained there, but draw in others by their pungency and heat. In this way too it benefits those suffering from enterocele. For it both dries and imparts tone to bodies that are slack through moisture, inasmuch as the astringency sinks down into the depth by being guided by the heat mixed with it, which has come to such a measure as to be able to guide, but not yet to be able to bite. Some also use it for carbuncles and for herpes, mixing it with barley-meal, so that it may consume, without heating, the moistures that produce such diseases. And some use it also against erysipelas, mixing it with barley-meal together with water or watery oxykraton.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκβόσκεται, ποδηγεῖν, ἐκδαπανώσῃ, μιγνύντες ἀλφίτου μεθ’ ὕδατος, σφαιρία
7.10.63 [63. On cypress.] The leaves of the cypress, its shoots, and its young, soft little balls are agglutinating of large wounds in hard bodies. From this it is clear that it is of a drying power, one that has nothing conspicuously pungent and hot, as indeed the taste also testifies. For in the taste the pungent appears to be slight, the bitter very great, and the acerbic much greater still throughout the whole plant. The pungent and the hot in it are only so much as to guide the acerbity into the depth, but not to produce any heat or biting in bodies. For this reason it feeds upon and consumes the moistures in the depth in flabby and putrefactive conditions, both painlessly and safely at once, whereas the heating and drying powers use up the moistures contained there but draw in others by their pungency and heat. In this way it also benefits those suffering from enterocele. For it both dries and imparts tone to bodies that are slack through moisture, since the astringency sinks down into the depth by being guided by the heat mixed with it, which reaches such a measure that it is able to guide but not yet able to bite. Some also use it for carbuncles and herpes, mixing it with barley-meal, on the ground that it uses up, without heating, the moistures that produce such diseases. Some also use it for erysipelas, mixing it with barley-meal together with water or watery oxycrate.

7.10.65 [65. On kypros.] Kypros. Of this tree the leaves and the young shoots come into use, being for the most part of a mixed power and mixture. Greek (Kühn)
7.10.68 [ξη΄. Περὶ κώνου.] Κώνου ὁ καρπὸς, ὃν δὴ καὶ κόκαλον ὀνομάζουσι καὶ στρόβιλον, ὅλος μὲν χλωρὸς, ἔχει τι μεθ’ ὑγρότητος πικρὸν καὶ δριμὺ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τοῖς ἐμπύοις, ὅσοι τ’ ἄλλοι δέονται τὰ κατὰ θώρακα καὶ πνεύμονα, ῥᾳδίως ἀναβήττειν τε καὶ ἀναπτύειν ἐπιτήδειός ἐστιν. ὁ δ’ ἐδώδιμος ἐξ αὐτοῦ καρπὸς ἔστι μὲν τροφὴ δύσπεπτός τε καὶ ἰσχυρὰ, ἔστι δὲ καὶ ὡς φάρμακον ἐκλεαίνειν τραχύτητας ἐπιτήδειον, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ἐν ὕδατι βρεχθεὶς ἀπόθηται κατ’ αὐτὸ πᾶν ὅσον ἔχει δριμύ. τὸ γὰρ λοιπὸν ἅπαν ἀδηκτότερόν τε καὶ ἐμπλαστικώτερον γίνεται, θερμῆς μὲν καὶ ψυχρᾶς ποιότητος ἐν τῷ μέσῳ καθεστηκὸς, ἐξ ὑδατώδους τε καὶ γεώδους οὐσίας κεκραμένον, ἀερώδους δ’ ἥκιστα μετέχον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κόκαλον
7.10.68 [68. On the pine (kōnos).] Of the pine (kōnos), the fruit — which indeed they also call kokalos and strobilos — when it is green as a whole has, together with moisture, something bitter and pungent, and for this reason it is suitable for those with internal suppuration (the empyic), and for all the others who need to cough up and expectorate easily the matter in the chest and lung. But the edible fruit from it is, on the one hand, a nourishment hard to digest and strong, and, on the other, as a drug it is suitable for smoothing roughnesses, and especially when, having been soaked in water, it has deposited in that water all the pungency it contains. For all the remainder becomes less biting and more clogging, standing in the middle between hot and cold quality, blended from watery and earthy substance, and partaking least of airy substance.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅλος μὲν χλωρὸς, ἀπόθηται κατ’ αὐτὸ
7.10.68 [68. On the pine cone.] The fruit of the pine cone (κῶνος), which they also call kokalos and strobilos, taken whole while green, has something bitter and pungent together with moistness, and for this reason it is suitable for the empyematous, and for any others who need to cough up and spit up easily what is in the chest and lung. The edible fruit that comes out of it is a nourishment both hard to digest and strong, but it is also suitable as a drug for smoothing roughnesses, especially when, steeped in water, it gives off into the water all the pungency it has. For all that remains becomes less biting and more clogging, being placed in the middle between hot and cold quality, mixed from a watery and an earthy substance, and having very little share of airy substance.

Περὶ λαθυρίδος.] Λαθυρίς. καὶ ταύτην τινὲς εἶδος τιθυμάλλου φασὶν, ὅτι τε παραπλησίως ἐκείνοις ὀπὸν ἔχει καὶ ὅτι καθαίρει τὸν αὐτὸν ἐκείνοις τρόπον, ὁμοία τε τὴν ὅλην δύναμιν ἐκείνοις ἐστὶ, πλὴν ὅσον τὸ σπέρμα γλυκὺ γευομένοις φαίνεται, ὃ δὴ καὶ μάλιστα τὴν καθαρτικὴν ἔχει δύναμιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.11.1 [1. On hare's-foot.] Hare's-foot is of a drying power, so that it dries up well the fluxes in the belly. 7.11.2 [2. On lathyris.] Lathyris. Some say that this too is a species of tithymallos, both because, in like manner to them, it has a juice and because it purges in the same way as they do, and it is similar to them in its power as a whole, except in so far as the seed appears sweet to those who taste it, and it is this indeed that most of all possesses the purgative power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
7.11.1 [1. On lagopous.] Lagopous (hare's-foot) is of a drying power, so that it dries up the fluxes in the belly well. 7.11.2 [2. On lathyris.] Lathyris. This too some say is a kind of tithymallus, both because it has a juice similar to theirs and because it purges in the same manner as they do; and it is similar to them in its whole power, except insofar as the seed appears sweet to those who taste it, and it is this, indeed, that has the purgative power most of all.

7.11.3 [γ΄. Περὶ λαμψάνης.] Λαμψάνη ἐσθιομένη μὲν κακόχυμός ἐστι, καταπλασσομένη δὲ ῥυπτικὸν ἔχει τι καὶ διαφορητικόν. 7.11.4 [δ΄. Περὶ λαπάθου.] Λάπαθον διαφορητικῆς μετρίως ἐστὶ δυνάμεως, τὸ δὲ ὀξυλάπαθον μικτῆς. ἅμα γὰρ τῇ διαφορητικῇ καὶ ἀποκρουστικῆς τι μετέχει. τὸ δὲ σπέρμα αὐτῶν ἔχει τι σαφῶς καὶ στυπτικὸν, ὡς καὶ δυσεντερίας καὶ διαῤῥοίας ἰᾶσθαι, καὶ μάλιστα τὸ τοῦ ὀξυλαπάθου. τὸ δ’ ἱππολάπαθον ἐν ἕλεσι μὲν γεννᾶται τοὐπίπαν· ἔστι δὲ τῆς αὐτῆς μὲν τοῖς εἰρημένοις, ἀσθενεστέρας δὲ δυνάμεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.11.3 [3. On lampsane.] Lampsane (charlock), when eaten, is productive of bad humour, but applied as a plaster it has something cleansing and dispersing. 7.11.4 [4. On dock (lapathon).] Dock (lapathon) is of a moderately dispersing power, but sorrel (oxylapathon) is of a mixed one; for together with the dispersing power it also partakes of something repelling. Their seed also has something clearly astringent, so that it cures both dysentery and diarrhoea, and especially that of sorrel (oxylapathon). The horse-dock (hippolapathon) grows for the most part in marshes; it is of the same power as those mentioned, but weaker.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λαμψάνης, Λαμψάνη
7.11.3 [3. On lampsane.] Lampsane, when eaten, is productive of bad humours, but when applied as a poultice it has something cleansing and dispersing. 7.11.4 [4. On docks.] Dock is of a moderately dispersing power, and oxylapathon (sharp dock) of a mixed one; for together with the dispersing power it also partakes of something repelling. Their seed clearly has something astringent as well, so that it cures dysenteries and diarrhoea, and especially that of oxylapathon. Hippolapathon (horse dock) grows for the most part in marshes; it is of the same power as those already mentioned, but of a weaker one.

Simp.7.11.6Simp. 7.11.6, ch. 7.11 difficulty high (6.74/100; thin 5, absent 1) (a) pass (b) fail 5/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
7.11.6 [στ΄. Περὶ λειχῆνος.] Λειχὴν ὁ ἐπὶ τῶν πετρῶν ἔστι μὲν οἷον βρύον τι, τοῦ γένους δ’ ἂν ὀρθῶς ὀνομάζοιτο τῶν φυτῶν. ὠνομάσθαι δ’ οὕτω δοκεῖ διὰ τὸ λειχῆνας θεραπεύειν. ἔστι δὲ ῥυπτικῆς τε ἅμα καὶ μετρίως ψυχούσης δυνάμεως, ξηραντικῆς μέντοι κατ’ ἄμφω. τὸ μὲν δὴ ῥυπτικόν τε καὶ ξηραντικὸν ἀπὸ τῆς πέτρας ἴσχει, τὸ δὲ ψυκτικὸν ἀπὸ τοῦ ὑδατώδους. ἐπὶ γὰρ ταῖς ὑγραῖς καὶ δροσώδεσι πέτραις φύεται. τὸ δ’ ἐκ τῶν τοιούτων οὐσιῶν συγκείμενον ἀφλέγμαντον ὑπάρχειν οὐδὲν θαυμαστόν. εἰ δὲ καὶ αἱμοῤῥαγίας ἵστησι, ὡς Διοσκορίδης φησὶν, οὐκ ἔχω φάναι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
7.11.6 [6. On lichen.] Lichen, the one on rocks, is indeed a sort of bryon (tree-moss), but it would correctly be named as belonging to the genus of plants. It seems to have been so named because it cures lichens. It is of a cleansing and at the same time moderately cooling power, yet drying in both respects. The cleansing and drying quality it has from the rock, the cooling from the watery; for it grows on moist and dewy rocks. And that what is composed of substances of this kind should be anti-inflammatory is nothing to wonder at. But whether it also stops haemorrhages, as Dioscorides says, I am not able to say.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοῦ γένους δ’ ἂν ὀρθῶς ὀνομάζοιτο τῶν φυτῶν, ἀφλέγμαντον
7.11.6 [6. On leichen.] The leichen that grows on rocks is like a kind of bryon, but it would rightly be named as belonging to the class of plants. It seems to have been so named because it treats leichenes. It is of a cleansing and at the same time moderately cooling power, but drying in both respects. It takes its cleansing and drying quality from the rock, and its cooling quality from the watery component. For it grows on moist and dewy rocks. It is nothing surprising that what is composed of such substances is preventive of inflammation. But whether it also stops haemorrhages, as Dioscorides says, I am not able to say.

Greek (Kühn)
7.11.11 [ια΄. Περὶ λευκοΐου.] Λευκοΐου καὶ σύμπας μὲν ὁ θάμνος ῥυπτικῆς ἐστι καὶ λεπτομεροῦς δυνάμεως, ἐπὶ μᾶλλον δ’ αὐτῆς μετέχει τὰ ἄνθη, καὶ τούτων τὰ ξηρότερα τῶν χλωρῶν μᾶλλον, ὥστε καὶ τὰς ἐπ’ ὀφθαλμοῖς οὐλὰς τὰς παχείας λεπτύνει καὶ καταμήνιά τε τὸ ἀφέψημα αὐτῶν προτρέπει καὶ χόρια καὶ ἔμβρυα τεθνεῶτα προκαλεῖται, καὶ εἰ ποθείη δὲ, φθόριόν ἐστι φάρμακον. καὶ γὰρ οὖν καὶ πικρόν ἐστιν, εἴπερ τι καὶ ἄλλο, καὶ εἴ τις αὐτοῦ τὸ σφοδρὸν τῆς δυνάμεως ὕδατος ἐπιμιξίᾳ πολλοῦ πραΰνειεν, ἤ τινι τῶν τοιούτων, ἕξει καὶ πρὸς φλεγμονὴν ἀγαθὸν φάρμακον. οὕτω γοῦν καὶ τὸ ἀφέψημα αὐτοῦ, εἰ μὴ ἄκρατον εἴη, τὰς ἐν μήτρᾳ φλεγμονὰς ἰᾶται προσαντλούμενον, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅσαι κεχρονίκασι σκιῤῥωδῶς. οὕτω δὲ καὶ μετὰ κηρωτῆς ἕλκη δυσεπούλωτα θεραπεύει. χρῶνται δ’ αὐτῷ τινες σὺν μέλιτι καὶ πρὸς ἄφθας. ὁ δὲ καρπὸς τῆς αὐτῆς ὢν δυνάμεως ἐπιτηδειότατός ἐστι πινόμενος ὅσον δυοῖν δραχμῶν πλῆθος ἢ προστιθέ μενος σὺν μέλιτι, καταμήνιά τε κινεῖν πεπίστευται καὶ ἔμβρυα τὰ μὲν ζῶντα διαφθείρειν, τὰ νεκρὰ δ’ ἐκβάλλειν. αἱ δὲ ῥίζαι παραπλησίας οὖσαι δυνάμεως, πλὴν ὅσον παχυμερέστεραί τ’ εἰσὶ καὶ γεωδέστεραι, μετ’ ὄξους ἰῶνται σπλῆνας σκιῤῥουμένους. ἔνιοι δὲ καὶ τὰς σκιῤῥουμένας φλεγμονὰς ἰῶνται δι’ αὐτοῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσαντλούμενον, χόρια
7.11.11 [11. On leukoion.] Of the leukoion the whole bush, too, is of a cleansing and fine-particled power, but the flowers partake of it more, and of these the drier ones more than the fresh, so that it also thins the thick scars on the eyes, and their decoction promotes the menses and calls forth the chorion and dead embryos, and if it be drunk, it is an abortifacient drug. For indeed it is also bitter, if anything is, and if one should soften the vehemence of its power by admixture of much water, or by one of such things, one will have a good drug against inflammation as well. Thus at any rate its decoction too, if it be not undiluted, cures inflammations in the womb when applied as a fomentation, and especially those that have become chronic in a scirrhous manner. Thus also with a cerate it treats ulcers hard to cicatrize. Some use it with honey also against aphthae. The fruit, being of the same power, is most suitable when drunk in the amount of two drachms or applied as a pessary with honey; it is believed to move the menses and to destroy living embryos and to expel dead ones. The roots, being of a similar power, except insofar as they are more coarse-particled and more earthy, with vinegar cure spleens that are becoming scirrhous. Some also cure scirrhous inflammations by means of it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπιτηδειότατός ἐστι πινόμενος (the construction breaks off: 'most suitable' for what is not stated before πεπίστευται), προστιθέμενος (taken as 'applied as a pessary'), προσαντλούμενον, δι’ αὐτοῦ (singular: referent unclear, whether the vinegar, the root, or the plant), Λευκοΐου (plant name left as 'leukoion')
7.11.11 [11. On leukoion.] Of leukoion the whole shrub is of a cleansing and fine-particled power, but the flowers share in it to a greater degree, and of these the drier more than the fresh, so that they even thin the thick scars on the eyes, and their decoction promotes the menses and draws out afterbirths and dead embryos, and if it is drunk, it is an abortifacient drug. For indeed it is also bitter, if anything else is, and if one should soften the vehemence of its power by mixing in much water, or something of that kind, one will have a good drug also for inflammation. Thus, at any rate, its decoction too, if it is not undiluted, heals inflammations in the womb when poured over them, and especially those that have become chronic in a scirrhous manner. And thus also, with a cerate, it treats ulcers that are hard to cicatrize. Some also use it with honey for aphthae. The fruit, being of the same power, is most suitable when drunk in an amount of two drachms or applied as a pessary with honey; it is believed both to move the menses and to destroy living embryos and expel dead ones. The roots, being of a similar power, except insofar as they are more coarse-particled and more earthy, heal indurated (scirrhous) spleens with vinegar. And some also heal indurating (scirrhous) inflammations by means of it.

7.11.13 [ιγ΄. Περὶ λιβανωτοῦ.] Λιβανωτὸς θερμαντικὸς μέν ἐστι κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τάξιν, ξηραντικὸς δὲ κατὰ τὴν πρώτην. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ ὑποστῦφον ὀλίγον, ἥκιστα δὲ σαφὴς ἡ στύψις ἐστὶν ἐν τῷ λευκῷ κατὰ τὴν χρόαν. ὁ δὲ φλοιὸς αὐτοῦ τὴν στυπτικὴν δύναμιν ἐναργῶς κέκτηται, διὸ καὶ ξηραίνει γενναίως, ὡς ἐν τῇ δευτέρᾳ τάξει συμπληρουμένῃ τῶν ξηραινόντων ὑπάρχων. ἔστι δὲ καὶ παχυμερέστερος τοῦ λιβανωτοῦ καὶ ἥκιστα δριμύτητος μετέχων. διὰ ταύτας γοῦν αὐτοῦ τὰς ποιότητάς τε καὶ δυνάμεις ἱκανὴ χρῆσίς ἐστι παρὰ τοῖς ἰατροῖς, ἐπί τε τῶν αἱμοπτυϊκῶν καὶ στομαχικῶν καὶ κοιλιακῶν καὶ δυσεντερικῶν, οὐ τοῖς ἔξωθεν μόνοις ἐπιτιθεμένοις μιγνύντων αὐτὸν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς εἴσω τοῦ σώματος μεταλαμβανομένοις. ὁ δὲ θαλλὸς αὐτοῦ ξηροτέρας ἐστὶ καὶ θερμοτέρας ἢ κατὰ αὐτὸν τὸν λιβανωτὸν δυνάμεως, ὡς εἰς τὴν τρίτην ἀπόστασιν ἀφικνεῖσθαι. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ ῥυπτικὸν, ὥστε διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ἀνακαθαίρειν δοκεῖ καὶ πληροῦν τὰ κατὰ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς ἕλκη, καθάπερ καὶ ὁ ἐκ τῆς σμύρνης καὶ ὁ ἐκ τοῦ στύρακος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: θαλλὸς, συμπληρουμένῃ, ἀπόστασιν
7.11.13 [13. On frankincense.] Frankincense is heating in the second rank, and drying in the first. It also has something slightly astringent, but the astringency is least evident in that which is white in colour. Its bark, however, clearly possesses the astringent power, and for this reason it also dries vigorously, since it stands in the second rank of drying agents at its full extent. It is also more coarse-particled than frankincense and partakes least of pungency. On account of these qualities and powers of it, then, there is considerable use of it among physicians, in the case of those who spit blood and of stomach patients, coeliac patients and dysenterics, who mix it not only into the remedies applied externally but also into those taken into the interior of the body. Its shoot is of a drier and hotter power than frankincense itself, so as to reach the third degree. It also has something cleansing, so that for this reason it seems both to clean out and to fill up the ulcers in the eyes, just as do that from myrrh and that from storax.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: θαλλὸς (rendered 'shoot'; the comparison with the products 'from myrrh' and 'from storax' suggests a corruption, possibly for a word meaning the soot of frankincense), συμπληρουμένῃ (rendered 'at the completion of' the second order), ἀπόστασιν (rendered 'remove', i.e. degree), στομαχικῶν (rendered 'stomachic patients'), κοιλιακῶν (rendered 'coeliac patients')
7.11.13 [13. On frankincense.] Frankincense is heating in the second order and drying in the first. It also has something slightly astringent, though the astringency is least evident in the kind that is white in colour. Its bark, however, plainly possesses the astringent power, and for this reason it also dries vigorously, being among the drying things at the completion of the second order. It is also more coarse-particled than the frankincense and partakes of pungency very little. Because of these qualities and powers of it, physicians make considerable use of it for those who spit blood, stomachic, coeliac and dysenteric patients, mixing it not only into things applied externally but also into things taken inside the body. Its shoot is of a drier and hotter power than frankincense itself, so as to reach the third remove. It also has something cleansing, so that for this reason it is thought both to clean out and to fill up ulcers in the eyes, as do also that from myrrh and that from storax.

7.11.14 [ιδ΄. Περὶ λιβανωτίδων.] Λιβανωτίδες τρεῖς εἰσι· μία ﻿ μὲν ἄκαρπος, αἱ δὲ δύο φέρουσαι καρπὸν, ὁμοίας ἅπασαι δυνάμεως μαλακτικῆς τε καὶ διαφορητικῆς. ὁ δὲ χυλὸς ὅ τε τῆς ῥίζης καὶ ὁ τῆς πόας μέλιτι μιγνύμενος ἀμβλυωπίας, ὅσαι διὰ πάχος ὑγρῶν γίνονται θεραπεύει. τῆς δὲ εἰς τοὺς στεφάνους χρησίμης λιβανωτίδος, ἣν Ῥωμαῖοι καλοῦσι ῥουσμαρῖνον, τὸ ἀφέψημα τοὺς ἰκτεριῶντας ὀνίνησι πινόμενον. μετέχουσι γὰρ αἱ λιβανωτίδες ἅπασαι ῥυπτικῆς τε καὶ τμητικῆς δυνάμεως.

7.11.14 [14. On libanotides.] There are three libanotides: one is fruitless, while the two others bear fruit, and all are of similar power, softening and dispersing. The juice both of the root and of the herb, mixed with honey, cures the dimnesses of sight that arise from thickness of the humours. Of the libanotis useful for garlands, which the Romans call rusmarinum, the decoction, when drunk, benefits those suffering from jaundice. For all the libanotides share in a cleansing and cutting power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥουσμαρῖνον, ὑγρῶν
7.11.14 [14. On the libanotides.] There are three libanotides: one is fruitless, while the other two bear fruit, and all are of a similar power, softening and dispersing. The juice, both that of the root and that of the herb, mixed with honey, treats amblyopias, those that arise from thickness of fluids. Of the libanotis that is useful for garlands, which the Romans call rusmarinum, the decoction, when drunk, benefits the icteric. For all the libanotides share in a cleansing and cutting power.
